Forgotten Password Cancel Home / Featured Content / Seeking a Lasting Cure for Hepatitis B Seeking a Lasting Cure for Hepatitis B For millions of people living with chronic hepatitis B, long term treatment has been the only option. EU funded researchers are joining forces to develop combination therapy that could one day free patients from lifelong medication. August 1, 2026 by Horizon Magazine Leave a Comment By Maria Vlastara Hepatitis B is a chronic infection affecting roughly 254 million people worldwide and causing about 1.1 million deaths each year, mainly from cirrhosis and liver cancer. Although a preventive vaccine has been available for more than 40 years, there is still no cure for people already living with the disease. Current antiviral medicines can suppress the virus, but cannot eliminate it. According to the European Centre for Disease Prevention and Control (ECDC), around 3.6 million people in the 27 EU countries, Iceland, Liechtenstein and Norway are chronically infected with hepatitis B. Many do not realise they are infected until serious liver damage has already occurred, leaving them on lifelong treatment. “People think we have solved the problem because we have a preventive vaccine. But for the millions already living with chronic hepatitis B, that is simply not true,” said Professor Ulrike Protzer, director of the Institute of Virology at the Technical University of Munich and Helmholtz Munich. Now, however, Protzer and other researchers across Europe believe a functional cure may finally be within reach. Protzer has spent more than two decades studying how hepatitis B evades the immune system, and developing new ways to help the body fight back. “I call hepatitis B the most frequent neglected disease in the world,” said Protzer, who is leading an international team of researchers developing a new vaccine for people living with chronic hepatitis B. Their work is part of the six‐year EU‐funded TherVacB research initiative that concludes at the end of 2026. While conventional hepatitis B vaccines prevent infection, this therapeutic vaccine is intended for people who are already chronically infected. I call hepatitis B the most frequent neglected disease in the world. Video Player is loading. Advertisement Next Stay Close ✕ Ad loading Don’t like ads? Become a supporter and enjoy The Good Men Project ad free Rather than trying to attack the virus directly, Protzer’s team is asking a different question: can the immune system itself be trained to regain control? The aim is to recreate the type of immune response seen in people who naturally clear hepatitis B after becoming infected later in life. “People who eliminate the virus develop strong antibody and T‐cell responses,” said Protzer, referring to immune cells that detect and kill infected cells. “We designed our therapeutic vaccine to activate exactly those kinds of immune responses.” Early clinical studies in healthy volunteers showed the vaccine was well tolerated and successfully stimulated the expected immune response. Patient trials are now underway in Germany, Italy, Spain, the UK and Tanzania. While the researchers caution that the work remains in its early clinical stages, they believe therapeutic vaccines could eventually become an important component of future combination treatments. Cause for hope The World Health Organization aims to eliminate viral hepatitis as a public health threat by 2030. Achieving that goal will require earlier diagnosis, wider vaccination and more effective treatments for people already living with chronic infection. Growing optimism in recent years comes from a shift in scientific thinking. Rather than relying on a single treatment to eliminate the virus, researchers increasingly believe that a functional cure will come from combining therapies that work effectively together, allowing patients to keep the virus under control without continuous treatment. “We are entering a very exciting time in hepatitis B research,” said Dr Maria Butí Ferret, a hepatologist with decades of experience treating patients with the disease. “Many new treatments are being developed, targeting different parts of the virus or helping the immune system fight it more effectively. The future will probably not come from a single treatment, but from combining different approaches that work together.” Butí Ferret is a professor of medicine at the Universitat Autònoma de Barcelona and a senior hepatologist at Hospital Universitari Vall d’Hebron, one of Europe’s leading liver disease centres. For the past six years, she has been part of another international research team supported by the EU called IP‐cure‐B, which investigates whether scientists can restore the immune system’s ability to control hepatitis B naturally. Current antiviral medicines cannot remove the tiny viral reservoir that remains hidden inside liver cells. As soon as treatment stops, the virus often returns. Researchers in IP‐cure‐B therefore explored whether they could “wake up” the body’s own defences before stopping antiviral medicine. In this way, the immune system would be better prepared to keep the virus under control in the long term. The project’s Phase IIa clinical trial has now been completed, with researchers analysing whether this immune‐based approach can achieve a functional cure. Alongside the clinical trial, the team is also searching for biological clues in patients’ blood and immune systems that could reveal who is most likely to benefit from future immune‐based treatments. “What we learn won’t only be relevant for our own study,” said project coordinator Fabien Zoulim, professor of medicine at Claude Bernard University Lyon 1, France, and one of Europe’s leading experts on chronic hepatitis B. “It could also help us understand which patients are most likely to respond to the new immune‐based treatments or direct acting antivirals that are now emerging.” Combining strengths Increasingly, scientists think that combination therapy will be the key. If the virus is first reduced using antiviral medicines or new antiviral technologies, the immune system may then be better able to respond to therapeutic vaccines or other immune‐based treatments. Precision medicine for hepatitis B will be a must to optimise the hepatitis B virus cure rates. Recognising that no single approach is likely to solve the problem alone, the TherVacB and IP‐cure‐B teams teamed up in 2023. They shared expertise and compared findings as they explored how different immune‐based strategies could ultimately be combined to improve patients’ chances of achieving long‐lasting relief. “If virus levels remain high, immune cells become overwhelmed,” Protzer explained. “The logical approach is first to reduce the amount of virus and then activate the immune response.” Zoulim believes these new approaches will also make treatment more personalised. “The objective is to better characterise patients and propose the best treatment according to their own specific situation,” he said. “Precision medicine for hepatitis B will be a must to optimise the hepatitis B virus cure rates.” Promise of a cure Recent clinical advances have strengthened that optimism. Several experimental therapies have shown that a functional cure is achievable in a proportion of patients, transforming expectations for a disease once considered incurable. “Living with a chronic infection is not only a medical issue, it can also have an emotional and social impact,” Butí Ferret said. “Many people still experience stigma and misunderstanding around hepatitis B.” A functional cure could mean fewer years of medication, less anxiety and a lower risk of liver disease progressing towards cirrhosis and cancer. But the researchers stress that scientific advances must go hand in hand with wider testing, earlier diagnosis and treatment, and continued vaccination. While no single therapy is expected to provide the answer, researchers believe that combining new antiviral and immune‐based approaches marks the start of a new chapter in hepatitis B research, shifting the focus from controlling the disease to curing it. Research in this article was funded by the EU’s Horizon Programme. The views of the interviewees don’t necessarily reflect those of the European Commission. Location and population
EU countries, Iceland, Liechtenstein, Norway, Germany, Italy, Spain, UK, Tanzania.
Event
Researchers are developing a new therapeutic vaccine for chronic hepatitis B, aiming to train the immune system to regain control of the virus. This is part of the EU-funded TherVacB initiative, which is in its sixth year and concludes in 2026. The vaccine is intended for people already chronically infected, unlike conventional vaccines that prevent infection.
Scale, impact and risk
Hepatitis B affects approximately 254 million people globally, causing about 1.1 million deaths annually, primarily from cirrhosis and liver cancer. Around 3.6 million people in the 27 EU countries, Iceland, Liechtenstein, and Norway are chronically infected. The current antiviral medicines can suppress the virus but cannot eliminate it, and the virus often returns after treatment stops.
Response, status and uncertainty
The therapeutic vaccine is in early clinical trials in several countries, and preliminary results show it is well tolerated and stimulates the expected immune response. However, the research remains in its early stages, and the effectiveness of the vaccine in treating chronic hepatitis B has not yet been confirmed. The EU-funded TherVacB initiative is ongoing, and the goal is to develop combination therapies for a functional cure.

加纳 75% 的肝癌病例是由乙型肝炎引起的 - 专家 - 现代加纳

Hepatitis B causes 75% of liver cancer cases in Ghana – Experts - Modern Ghana
新闻简报根据 Korle Bu 教学医院 2021 年的一项研究以及其他医疗机构的类似研究结果,加纳专家已确认该国 75% 的肝癌病例是乙型肝炎造成的。加纳肝脏和消化疾病研究协会 (GASLIDD) 主席 Eileen Akonobea Micah 博士强调,肝癌是加纳男性中第二大常见癌症,女性中第四大常见癌症,强调了其日益增长的公共卫生重要性。她对 45 岁的肝癌患者的平均年龄表示担忧,并指出许多人都是年轻且具有经济生产力的人。Micah 博士还指出,在医疗机构就诊晚是一个主要问题,通常会导致晚期诊断和有限的 治疗 选择。对此,她呼吁将乙型肝炎 治疗 服务的权力下放,类似于丙型肝炎的努力,以实现世界卫生组织的目标,即到 2030 年治疗至少 80% 的乙型和丙型肝炎患者。这份由现代加纳于 2026 年 8 月 3 日发表的报告基于专家分析和研究结果,没有提及未解决的信息。
查看新闻五要素
时间
加纳肝脏和消化疾病研究协会 (GASLIDD) 主席 Eileen Akonobea Micah 博士表示,加纳仍然是非洲肝癌负担最高的国家之一,这种疾病夺走了许多人​​在最富有生产力的岁月的生命。
地点与对象
加纳,这种疾病影响着全国各地的人们,特别是那些处于生产力最高的年份的人们。
事件
专家报告称,根据 Korle Bu 教学医院 2021 年的一项研究以及其他医疗机构的类似研究结果,加纳 75% 的肝癌病例是由乙型肝炎引起的。这是肝病专家证实的说法。
规模、影响与风险
乙型肝炎感染约占加纳肝癌病例的 75%,该疾病是男性中第二大常见癌症,女性中第四大常见癌症。在医疗机构中迟到就诊是一个重大挑战,导致晚期诊断和有限的 治疗 选择。
应对、状态与不确定性
Micah 博士呼吁分散乙型肝炎 治疗 服务,以改善获得 诊断 和护理的机会。她还提到了公共筛查、疫苗接种 和早期医疗护理的必要性。来源是完整的原始报告,调查结果基于研究和专家陈述。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

Hepatitis B causes 75% of liver cancer cases in Ghana – Experts - Modern Ghana

News Brief
Experts in Ghana have confirmed that hepatitis B is responsible for 75% of liver cancer cases in the country, according to a 2021 study at the Korle Bu Teaching Hospital and similar findings in other health facilities. Dr Eileen Akonobea Micah, President of the Ghana Association for the Study of Liver and Digestive Diseases (GASLIDD), highlighted that liver cancer is the second most common cancer among men and the fourth among women in Ghana, underscoring its growing public health significance. She expressed concern over the average age of 45 for liver cancer patients, noting that many are young and economically productive individuals. Dr Micah also pointed out that late presentation at health facilities is a major issue, often resulting in advanced-stage diagnoses and limited treatment options. In response, she called for the decentralisation of hepatitis B treatment services, similar to efforts for hepatitis C, to meet the World Health Organization's target of treating at least 80% of people living with hepatitis B and C by 2030. The report, published on August 3, 2026, by Modern Ghana, is based on expert analysis and study results, with no unresolved information mentioned.
Original source text (English)
Home Radio TV Videos Movies Advertise Ghana Contact Login NEWS SPORTS BUSINESS POLITICS OPINIONS HEALTH AFRICA WORLD ENTERTAINMENT LIFESTYLE SCIENCE TECHNOLOGY NOLLYWOOD MEDIA MEMBERS Archive Health News Family/Parenting Home/Food Health/Fitness Spirituality OnAIR Search Mon, 03 Aug 2026 Health Hepatitis B causes 75% of liver cancer cases in Ghana – Experts Mon, 03 Aug 2026 Hepatitis B infection accounts for about 75 per cent of liver cancer cases in Ghana, making it the leading cause of the disease and highlighting the urgent need to decentralise treatment services, liver disease specialists have said. Dr Eileen Akonobea Micah, President of the Ghana Association for the Study of Liver and Digestive Diseases (GASLIDD), said Ghana continued to record one of the highest liver cancer burdens in Africa, with the disease claiming the lives of many people in their most productive years. The Global Cancer Observatory (GLOBOCAN) 2024, indicates that liver cancer is the second most common cancer among men in Ghana, after prostate cancer, and the fourth most common among women, underscoring its growing public health significance. Dr Micah said this at the launch of World Hepatitis Day and the inauguration of a National Technical Working Group in Accra, held on the theme: “Hepatitis Elimination for Everyone, Everywhere, Right Now.” She said evidence from several studies conducted across the country consistently showed that chronic hepatitis B infection was responsible for the majority of liver cancer cases. She cited a 2021 study at the Korle Bu Teaching Hospital, which analysed patients attending the Gastrointestinal Clinic over a three-year period and found that three out of every four liver cancer cases (75 per cent) were linked to hepatitis B infection. She said similar findings had been recorded in several other health facilities across the country. Dr Micah expressed concern that many liver cancer patients were relatively young, with an average age of 45 years, depriving families and the nation of economically productive citizens. She recounted the case of two brothers in their late twenties who died of liver cancer caused by hepatitis B within three months of each other, while two of their siblings, who were also living with hepatitis B, were currently receiving treatment. She noted that many liver cancer patients were family breadwinners, and their deaths had significant social and economic consequences. Dr Micah also identified late presentation at health facilities as a major challenge, saying most patients were diagnosed only after the disease had reached an advanced stage, leaving them eligible only for palliative care. “Many survive for only three to six months after diagnosis because the cancer is detected too late for curative treatment,” she said. She, therefore, called for the decentralisation of hepatitis B treatment services, similar to ongoing efforts for hepatitis C, to improve access to diagnosis and care nationwide. Expanding treatment services to district and regional health facilities, she said, would help Ghana achieve the World Health Organization's target of treating at least 80 per cent of people living with hepatitis B and C by 2030, a key milestone towards eliminating viral hepatitis as a public health threat. Dr Micah reaffirmed GASLIDD's commitment to working with the Government and the Ministry of Health to strengthen hepatitis prevention and control efforts. She said the Association would continue to support initiatives aimed at improving linkage to care, expanding access to treatment, and training healthcare professionals in the prevention, diagnosis and management of hepatitis. Health experts urged the public to undergo hepatitis B screening, receive vaccination where eligible, and seek early medical care to prevent chronic infection from progressing to liver cancer. Hepatitis is an inflammation of the liver commonly caused by viral infections, excessive alcohol consumption or autoimmune diseases. The most common viral forms are hepatitis A, B and C, which affect the liver's ability to process nutrients and filter blood. Hepatitis B and C are blood-borne infections that can become chronic and lead to severe liver damage if left untreated. Common symptoms include jaundice (yellowing of the skin and eyes), fatigue and pain in the upper right abdomen. However, many people infected with the hepatitis virus experience no symptoms in the early stages. GNA Follow our WhatsApp channel for meaningful stories picked for your day. B 常规重要公共卫生新闻

慢性乙型肝炎 中的 MASLD 引发系统性风险 - EMJ

MASLD in Chronic Hepatitis B Raises Systemic Risks - EMJ
新闻简报来自中国北方的一项基于人群的 队列研究 研究在 13.7 年内追踪了 3,269 名患有 慢性乙型肝炎 的成年人,发现代谢功能障碍相关的脂肪肝病 (MASLD) 使心血管疾病的风险几乎增加了一倍,并使肝外癌症的风险增加了 50%。即使在 MASLD 似乎逆转后,心血管风险仍然大约是原来的两倍。患有 MASLD 的参与者的 12 年累积心血管事件 发病率 为 9.4%,而没有 MASLD 的参与者为 4.9%。作者建议将代谢和心血管监测纳入 慢性乙型肝炎 患者的常规护理中,包括肝酶正常或脂肪变性已解决的患者,同时注意到研究的局限性,例如缺乏纵向 HBV DNA 和 抗病毒药物 治疗 数据。
查看新闻五要素
时间
发布于 2026-08-06;研究期限 13.7 年(所提供的证据中未报告确切的事件日期)。
地点与对象
中国北方;基于人群的 队列研究,涉及 3,269 名感染 慢性乙型肝炎 病毒的成年人。
事件
一项基于人群的 队列研究 研究报告称,根据 13.7 年的随访,慢性乙型肝炎 患者的代谢功能障碍相关脂肪性肝病 (MASLD) 与心血管疾病和肝外癌症风险增加相关。
规模、影响与风险
基线处的 MASLD 患病率:26.5% (867/3,269)。调整后的风险增加:心血管疾病风险增加 98%,肝外癌症风险增加 50%。12 年累积心血管疾病 发病率:使用 MASLD 时为 9.4%,不使用 MASLD 时为 4.9%。患有 MASLD 的参与者记录了 36 例肝外癌症,而没有 MASLD 的参与者记录了 69 例。调整后,MASLD 与原发性肝癌不独立相关。
应对、状态与不确定性
研究局限性包括缺乏纵向 HBV DNA、抗病毒药物 治疗 和早期纤维化数据;通过超声检查评估肝脂肪变性;研究结果需要在其他种族和生活方式群体中进行验证。作者建议将病毒学控制和代谢健康纳入 慢性乙型肝炎 患者的长期临床评估和持续心血管监测中,包括丙氨酸转氨酶正常或肝脂肪变性已解决的患者。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

MASLD in Chronic Hepatitis B Raises Systemic Risks - EMJ

News Brief
A population-based cohort study from northern China tracking 3,269 adults with chronic hepatitis B over 13.7 years found that metabolic dysfunction-associated steatotic liver disease (MASLD) nearly doubled the risk of cardiovascular disease and raised extrahepatic cancer risk by 50%. Even after MASLD appeared to reverse, cardiovascular risk remained approximately twice as high. The 12-year cumulative incidence of cardiovascular events was 9.4% among participants with MASLD versus 4.9% without. The authors recommend integrating metabolic and cardiovascular monitoring into routine care for people with chronic hepatitis B, including those with normal liver enzymes or resolved steatosis, while noting study limitations such as the absence of longitudinal HBV DNA and antiviral treatment data.
Original source text (English)
Key Summary: - MASLD nearly doubled cardiovascular disease risk in people with chronic hepatitis B. - Extrahepatic cancer risk was 50% higher among participants with MASLD. - Cardiovascular risk persisted after MASLD appeared to reverse. The cookies that are categorised as "Necessary" are stored on your browser as they are essential for enabling the basic functionalities of the site.... These cookies help provide information on metrics such as the number of visitors, bounce rate, traffic source, etc. Performance cookies are used to understand and analyze the key performance indexes of the website which helps in delivering a better user experience for the visitors. MASLD in chronic hepatitis B was associated with higher cardiovascular and extrahepatic cancer risks over 13.7 years. The population-based cohort study included 3,269 adults with chronic hepatitis B virus infection from northern China. Researchers examined whether concurrent metabolic dysfunction associated steatotic liver disease influenced the development of primary liver cancer, extrahepatic cancers, and cardiovascular disease. At baseline, 26.5% of participants had MASLD. These individuals were more likely to be overweight and have hypertension, prediabetes, or Type 2 diabetes than participants without MASLD. After adjustment for lifestyle, metabolic, inflammatory, and liver related variables, MASLD was associated with a 98% higher risk of cardiovascular disease and a 50% higher risk of extrahepatic cancers. Cardiovascular outcomes included myocardial infarction, ischemic stroke, and hemorrhagic stroke. The 12-year cumulative incidence of cardiovascular disease was 9.4% among participants with MASLD, compared with 4.9% among those without MASLD. Elevated cardiovascular disease risk was observed even among participants with mild steatosis or normal alanine aminotransferase levels. This finding indicates that apparently limited liver involvement or normal liver enzyme results may not identify individuals with lower systemic risk. Cardiovascular risk also remained elevated among participants whose MASLD had reversed by follow up. Compared with individuals who remained free from MASLD, those with reversed MASLD had approximately twice the risk of cardiovascular disease. Participants with persistent MASLD had a similarly elevated risk. Researchers recorded 36 extrahepatic cancers among participants with MASLD and 69 among those without the condition. Persistent MASLD was associated with a 98% higher risk of extrahepatic cancers compared with persistent non-MASLD. However, MASLD was not independently associated with primary liver cancer after adjustment for lifestyle and metabolic variables. Instead, the joint analysis identified a synergistic interaction between chronic hepatitis B and cardiometabolic risk factors in primary liver cancer development. The findings support continued metabolic management and cardiovascular monitoring for people with chronic hepatitis B, including those with normal alanine aminotransferase levels or resolved hepatic steatosis. The authors emphasized that virological control and metabolic health should both form part of long-term clinical assessment. The study was limited by the absence of longitudinal hepatitis B virus DNA, antiviral treatment, and early fibrosis data. Hepatic steatosis was also assessed using ultrasonography, and the findings require validation in populations with different ethnic and lifestyle backgrounds. Reference Wang X et al. Concurrent MASLD and Long-term Outcomes in HBV-infected Individuals: A Population-based Cohort Study. Biomed Environ Sci. 2026;39(7):785-798. Time
Published 2026-08-06; study period 13.7 years (exact event dates not reported in the supplied evidence).
Location and population
Northern China; population-based cohort of 3,269 adults with chronic hepatitis B virus infection.
Event
A population-based cohort study reported that metabolic dysfunction-associated steatotic liver disease (MASLD) in people with chronic hepatitis B is associated with increased risks of cardiovascular disease and extrahepatic cancers, based on 13.7 years of follow-up.
Scale, impact and risk
MASLD prevalence at baseline: 26.5% (867/3,269). Adjusted risk increases: 98% higher cardiovascular disease risk and 50% higher extrahepatic cancer risk. 12-year cumulative cardiovascular incidence: 9.4% with MASLD vs 4.9% without. 36 extrahepatic cancers recorded among participants with MASLD vs 69 among those without. MASLD was not independently associated with primary liver cancer after adjustment.
Response, status and uncertainty
Study limitations include lack of longitudinal HBV DNA, antiviral treatment, and early fibrosis data; hepatic steatosis assessed by ultrasonography; findings require validation in other ethnic and lifestyle groups. Authors recommend integrating virological control and metabolic health into long-term clinical assessment and continued cardiovascular monitoring for people with chronic hepatitis B, including those with normal alanine aminotransferase or resolved hepatic steatosis.

乙型肝炎:一种可预防的疾病,但仍夺去生命 - thecitizen.co.tz

Hepatitis B: A preventable disease still claiming lives - thecitizen.co.tz
新闻简报坦桑尼亚新闻媒体《公民报》于 2026 年 8 月 3 日发表了一篇题为“乙型肝炎:一种可预防的疾病,但仍在夺走生命”的文章。虽然这篇文章强调,尽管乙型肝炎是可以预防的,但它仍然会导致死亡,但它没有提供具体的统计数据、地理重点或有关正在进行的公共卫生行动的详细信息。没有包含官方声明、病例数或风险评估,导致问题的规模和应对工作尚不清楚。缺乏具体数据意味着该报告更多的是作为一般意识提醒,而不是可操作的 暴发 信息来源。
查看新闻五要素
时间
发表于2026-08-03;该文章没有具体说明所讨论的任何乙型肝炎事件的日期。
地点与对象
该报告源自坦桑尼亚,但提供的证据中没有确定受乙型肝炎影响的特定地区、社区或人群。
事件
文章指出,乙型肝炎仍然是一种可预防的疾病,并继续夺走生命,但没有提供详细数据或官方确认。
规模、影响与风险
所提供的证据中没有提供病例数、死亡人数、暴露数据或明确的风险评估。
应对、状态与不确定性
该报告没有提及任何调查、测试、治疗、接触者追踪或其他应对措施,并且关键细节尚未解决。

Hepatitis B: A preventable disease still claiming lives - thecitizen.co.tz

News Brief
A recent article titled "Hepatitis B: A preventable disease still claiming lives" was published on 3 August 2026 by the Tanzanian news outlet The Citizen. While the piece underscores that hepatitis B continues to cause fatalities despite being preventable, it offers no specific statistics, geographic focus, or details about ongoing public‐health actions. No official statements, case counts, or risk assessments are included, leaving the scale of the problem and response efforts unclear. The lack of concrete data means the report serves more as a general awareness reminder than a source of actionable outbreak information.

B 常规重要公共卫生新闻

医生表示,如果忽视乙型、丙型肝炎,可能会导致肝功能衰竭 - 论坛报

Hepatitis B, C can cause liver failure if ignored, says doctor - The Tribune
新闻简报这篇文章于 2026 年 8 月 5 日发表在印度贾朗达尔的《论坛报》上,强调了未经治疗的乙型和丙型肝炎感染的风险,这些感染可能导致肝衰竭、肝硬化和肝癌。Hoshiarpur 利瓦萨医院胃肠病学、肝病学和肝脏疾病高级顾问 Mukesh Kumar 博士在健康意识会议上谈到了这些风险。他建议患有糖尿病、肥胖、脂肪肝、过量饮酒、有肝病家族史或有未经筛查的输血史的人定期进行肝脏健康检查,以便及早发现。Kumar博士还指出,通过疫苗接种可以有效预防乙型肝炎,而现代抗病毒药物 治疗已经使丙型肝炎在大多数情况下可以治愈。这篇文章只是一篇健康意识文章,并未提供具体病例或死亡人数。内容不完整,来源为知名媒体。
查看新闻五要素
时间
文章发表于2026-08-05。证据中没有明确提及事件日期。
地点与对象
这篇文章来自印度贾朗达尔发表的《论坛报》。受影响的人群包括患有糖尿病、肥胖症、脂肪肝病、过量饮酒、肝病家族史或未经筛查的输血史的个体。
事件
Hoshiarpur 利瓦萨医院胃肠病学、肝病学和肝病高级顾问 Mukesh Kumar 博士讨论了未经治疗的乙型肝炎和丙型肝炎感染的风险,包括肝衰竭、肝硬化和肝癌。该活动是一次健康意识会议,并根据证据进行确认。
规模、影响与风险
该文章报告称,未经治疗的乙型和丙型肝炎可能会导致严重的健康后果,例如肝功能衰竭、肝硬化和肝癌。它还强调了定期肝脏健康检查对高危人群的重要性。证据中没有量化规模和影响。
应对、状态与不确定性
Kumar 博士强调了预防保健,包括针对乙型肝炎的 疫苗接种 和针对丙型肝炎的现代 抗病毒药物 治疗。响应状态基于建议和认识,来源是来自知名媒体的部分内容文章。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

Hepatitis B, C can cause liver failure if ignored, says doctor - The Tribune

News Brief
The article, published on August 5, 2026, by The Tribune in Jalandhar, India, highlights the risks of untreated Hepatitis B and C infections, which can lead to liver failure, cirrhosis, and liver cancer. Dr Mukesh Kumar, a Senior Consultant in Gastroenterology, Hepatology, and Liver Diseases at Livasa Hospital, Hoshiarpur, spoke during a health awareness session about these risks. He advised individuals with diabetes, obesity, fatty liver disease, excessive alcohol consumption, family history of liver disease, or a history of unscreened blood transfusions to undergo regular liver health check-ups for early detection. Dr Kumar also noted that Hepatitis B can be effectively prevented through vaccination, while modern antiviral treatments have made Hepatitis C curable in most cases. The article serves as a health awareness piece and does not provide specific case or death counts. The content is partial, and the source is a reputable media outlet.
Original source text (English)
Viral hepatitis, particularly Hepatitis B and Hepatitis C, continues to pose a major public health challenge as untreated infections can silently damage the liver for years before symptoms appear, said Dr Mukesh Kumar, Senior Consultant (Gastroenterology, Hepatology and Liver Diseases) at Livasa Hospital, Hoshiarpur. Speaking during a health awareness session, Dr Kumar said prolonged infections could gradually progress to liver cirrhosis, liver failure and even liver cancer if left untreated. He advised people not to ignore symptoms such as persistent fatigue, yellowing of the skin or eyes, dark urine, pale stools, abdominal discomfort, nausea, abdominal swelling, unexplained weight loss, itching and prolonged fever. Dr Kumar said individuals with diabetes, obesity, fatty liver disease, excessive alcohol consumption, family history of liver disease or a history of unscreened blood transfusions should undergo regular liver health check-ups for early detection of disease. He noted that Hepatitis B can be effectively prevented through vaccination, while modern antiviral medicines have made Hepatitis C curable in the vast majority of patients. Emphasising preventive care, Dr Kumar urged people to maintain a healthy diet, keep their weight under control, limit alcohol consumption, practise good hygiene and seek timely medical advice to protect liver health
View five news elements
Time
The article was published on 2026-08-05. The event date is not explicitly mentioned in the evidence.
Location and population
The article is from The Tribune, published in Jalandhar, India. The affected population includes individuals with diabetes, obesity, fatty liver disease, excessive alcohol consumption, family history of liver disease, or a history of unscreened blood transfusions.
Event
Dr Mukesh Kumar, a Senior Consultant in Gastroenterology, Hepatology, and Liver Diseases at Livasa Hospital, Hoshiarpur, discussed the risks of untreated Hepatitis B and Hepatitis C infections, including liver failure, cirrhosis, and liver cancer. The event is a health awareness session and is confirmed based on the evidence.
Scale, impact and risk
The article reports that untreated Hepatitis B and C can lead to severe health outcomes such as liver failure, cirrhosis, and liver cancer. It also highlights the importance of regular liver health check-ups for high-risk individuals. The scale and impact are not quantified in the evidence.
Response, status and uncertainty
Dr Kumar emphasized preventive care, including vaccination for Hepatitis B and modern antiviral treatments for Hepatitis C. The response status is based on recommendations and awareness, and the source is a partial content article from a reputable media outlet.

B 常规重要公共卫生新闻

乙型肝炎:一项新举措如何拯救新生儿免受感染 - standardmedia.co.ke

Hepatitis B: How a new initiative is saving newborns from infection - standardmedia.co.ke
新闻简报肯尼亚的一项新举措在预防乙型肝炎母婴 传播 方面取得了成功。检测呈阳性的孕妇接受 抗病毒药物 治疗 来抑制病毒,分娩期间的密切监测进一步降低了 传播 风险。在巴林戈县和基苏木县,参与该计划的乙型肝炎阳性母亲所生的所有 73 名婴儿的病毒检测均为阴性。该举措包括在第一次产前检查时对艾滋病毒、梅毒和乙型肝炎进行普遍三重筛查,确保早期发现和干预。公共卫生专家强调,如果按时实施,该计划将非常有效。
查看新闻五要素
时间
发表于2026-08-02;该事件被描述为发生在 2025 年初和 2025 年怀孕期间。
地点与对象
肯尼亚,特别是巴林戈县和基苏木县;到产前诊所就诊的孕妇及其新生儿。
事件
据报道,一项预防乙型肝炎母婴 传播 的新举措非常有效,孕妇接受 抗病毒药物 治疗 治疗并在分娩期间进行密切监测。该事件被描述为公共卫生干预措施,而不是 暴发。
规模、影响与风险
在巴林戈和基苏木参与该计划的 73 名乙型肝炎阳性母亲所生的婴儿中,病毒检测结果全部呈阴性。该举措包括在第一次产前检查时对所有孕妇进行三重筛查(艾滋病毒、梅毒、乙型肝炎)。
应对、状态与不确定性
该举措包括针对乙型肝炎阳性母亲的产前筛查、抗病毒药物 治疗 以及分娩期间的密切监测。所有登记母亲所生的婴儿检测结果均为阴性,表明有效性很高。所提供的证据中没有报告未解决的信息或限制。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

Hepatitis B: How a new initiative is saving newborns from infection - standardmedia.co.ke

News Brief
A new initiative in Kenya is demonstrating success in preventing mother-to-child transmission of hepatitis B. Pregnant women testing positive receive antiviral treatment to suppress the virus, and close monitoring during delivery further reduces transmission risk. In Baringo and Kisumu counties, all 73 babies born to hepatitis B-positive mothers enrolled in the program tested negative for the virus. The initiative includes universal triple screening for HIV, syphilis, and hepatitis B at the first antenatal visit, ensuring early detection and intervention. Public health experts highlight the program's high effectiveness when implemented on time.
Original source text (English)

ESTATE DIGGER TRIBUTES LIVE: KTN LIVE: Radio Maisha LIVE: Spice FM LIVE: Berur FM Hepatitis B: How a new initiative is saving newborns from infection Health & Science By Mercy Kahenda | 4d ago | 5 min read Infectious Diseases Pregnant women living with hepatitis B can safely give birth to virus-free babies.[Courtesy] Babies & Toddlers Every day during her pregnancy, Salima Sitet asked medics the same question: “Will my baby be born free of hepatitis B?” It was in early 2025, just months after her elder brother died from hepatitis B virus complications. At the time, Sitet was five months pregnant and attending her first antenatal clinic (ANC) visit. It is during this ANC visit that she was tested for hepatitis B and told she was positive. “I went into shock after testing positive,” recalls the 28-year-old mother of three from Maji Moto, in Baringo. “I was mourning my brother, only to test positive for the disease that had killed him. I didn’t know how to fight for my life, and that of my unborn child. I was scared,” she said. Pregnancy & Maternity After counselling, hepatitis B treatment was initiated to suppress the virus, reducing the risk of transmitting it to her unborn child. A week to delivery, she went to Marigat Sub County Hospital, where she was admitted for close monitoring to reduce the chances of the baby acquiring the virus during delivery. After delivery, the baby received the hepatitis B birth-dose vaccine within 24 hours, followed by the three doses of the pentavalent vaccine at 6, 10 and 14 weeks to complete the immunisation schedule. Dr Missiani Ochwoto, a virologist and medical research scientist at Kenya Medical Research Institute (KEMRI), explains that babies born to hepatitis B, infected mothers are exposed to the virus, and a hepatitis B birth-dose vaccine is given to the newborn within 24 hours of birth to protect babies, before they receive the regular pentavalent vaccine. When given on time, it is highly effective at preventing mother-to-child transmission of hepatitis B. The birth dose is often given together with hepatitis B immunoglobulin (HBIG), to provide additional protection. Infectious Diseases At nine months, Sitet’s daughter was tested and found to be hepatitis B-free. Today, at one year and four months, she remains free of the virus. This was the answer Sitet had prayed for throughout her pregnancy. “I was so happy,” the mother tells The Standard with a smile. With suppressed viral load, she can breastfeed the baby exclusively, now one year old. The Standard found Sitet at her home, cuddling and playing with her daughter. “I never wanted my baby to grow up depending on pills as I do,” she says. After she tested positive for the virus, her husband and their two other children were also screened. All the tests turned negative. They were then vaccinated against the disease. As Kenya joins the globe in marking this year’s World Hepatitis Day to be commemorated in Baringo, Sitet is among mothers demonstrating that, with early diagnosis and treatment, women living with hepatitis B can give birth to babies free of the virus. Babies & Toddlers “After everything I have gone through, knowing my baby is free of hepatitis B has given me hope in the fight against the disease,” she says. Sitet hopes more pregnant women will be tested early enough and put on medication to prevent transmitting the virus to newborns. However, according to the locals, stigma is the main hitch in the elimination of hepatitis B in Baringo, a county with a prevalence of 10.8 per cent. The disease in the region is confused with HIV, as positive patients are put on Antiviral (ARVs). “People think hepatitis B is a death sentence. But if you are tested early, take your medication and follow the doctors’ advice, you can give birth to a healthy baby,” she says. Sitet’s story is at the heart of Kenya’s growing effort to eliminate mother-to-child transmission of hepatitis B through early screening, treatment and birth-dose vaccination, through a campaign dubbed a clean generation. Clean Generation is a KEMRI and National AIDS and STI Control Programme (NASCOP)-led initiative aimed at preventing mother-to-child transmission of hepatitis B. Health Conditions The initiative, implemented in Baringo and Kisumu counties, combines screening pregnant women for hepatitis B, treating infected mothers to suppress the virus, and administering a hepatitis B birth-dose vaccine within 24 hours after delivery. Researchers say the initiative is showing encouraging results. For instance, of the 73 babies born to hepatitis B-positive mothers enrolled in the initiative in Baringo and Kisumu, all have tested negative for the virus. “We started this campaign so that we ensure there is a clean generation of babies born to hepatitis B-positive mothers. These are children born free of hepatitis B despite their mothers being hepatitis B positive,” says Dr Ochwoto, KEMRI researcher. He says they started the initiative in Baringo and Kisumu after observing that some children, especially in Baringo, are infected with hepatitis B despite receiving the vaccine at six weeks of age, suggesting many infections occur before the first routine dose is administered. The scientist explains that while Kenya’s childhood hepatitis B vaccination programme is highly effective, babies born to infected mothers are vulnerable during the first six weeks of life unless they receive a birth-dose vaccine within 24 hours after birth. Child Care “Together with hospital delivery and a birth-dose vaccine, this ensures babies are born free of hepatitis B,” says Dr Ochwoto. Research on Hepatitis B followed investigations in Marigat, Baringo County in 2017, after clinicians noticed unusually high numbers of liver cancer patients from the area. KEMRI subsequently screened more than 4,000 people, including over 3,000 from Marigat, identifying more than 200 hepatitis B infections. Those diagnosed were linked to care, while about 120 people with high viral loads achieved viral suppression following treatment, reducing their risk of transmitting the virus and developing liver cancer. The Ministry of Health, through NASCOP, has come up with integrated guidelines for HIV, Hepatitis and STIs of 2026. The guidelines envision screening and scaling up treatment for those who are diagnosed with Hepatitis B. Head of Viral Hepatitis and STIs at NASCOP, Dr Hadija Hassan, says they are spearheading scale-up and accessibility of treatment, screening, and prevention of Hepatitis. Counseling Services “We want the generation that is coming to be hepatitis B free,” adds the NASCOP official. A visit to Marigat Hospital revealed that every pregnant woman attending her first antenatal clinic is offered triple screening for HIV, syphilis and hepatitis B. At the facility is Gladys Tutoek, Baringo County’s Prevention of Mother-to-Child Transmission (PMTCT) coordinator, who notes that eliminating mother-to-child transmission of HIV, syphilis and hepatitis B begins with early ANC visits. “Women who test positive for hepatitis B are started on treatment from around 28 weeks of pregnancy to suppress the viral load and reduce the risk of transmitting the virus to their babies,” Tuitoek says in an interview. At the facility, healthcare workers also closely monitor mothers on treatment, provide counselling and link them to support groups to improve adherence and reduce stigma. Kenya, specifically Baringo and Kisumu counties; pregnant women attending antenatal clinics and their newborns.
Event
A new initiative to prevent mother-to-child transmission of hepatitis B is reported as effective, with pregnant women receiving antiviral treatment and close monitoring during delivery. The event is described as a public health intervention, not an outbreak.
Scale, impact and risk
Of 73 babies born to hepatitis B-positive mothers enrolled in the initiative in Baringo and Kisumu, all tested negative for the virus. The initiative includes triple screening (HIV, syphilis, hepatitis B) for all pregnant women at first antenatal visit.
Response, status and uncertainty
The initiative involves antenatal screening, antiviral treatment for hepatitis B-positive mothers, and close monitoring during delivery. All babies born to enrolled mothers tested negative, indicating high effectiveness. No unresolved information or limitations are reported in the supplied evidence.

早期筛查如何击败“无声杀手”乙型和丙型肝炎

How early screening can beat 'silent killers' Hepatitis B and C
新闻简报A leading Indian hepatologist has underscored the critical role of early screening in combating Hepatitis B virus (HBV) infections, which often progress silently for years before causing severe liver damage such as cirrhosis or cancer. Dr. Vikram Raut, a senior consultant at Fortis Hospitals Mumbai, notes that 300 million Indians may unknowingly live with HBV or Hepatitis C, with symptoms like fatigue or nausea frequently misattributed to stress or lifestyle factors. HBV is preventable via vaccination and controllable with antiviral therapy, while Hepatitis C—transmitted mainly through contaminated blood—can often be cured with short-course oral medicines. The article urges high-risk groups, including healthcare workers and dialysis patients, to seek screening, warning that delayed diagnosis dramatically increases the risk of life-threatening complications.
查看新闻五要素
时间
发表于2026-08-06;所提供的证据中没有报告具体的事件日期。
地点与对象
印度;普通人群,重点关注高危人群,如医护人员、有乙型肝炎家族史的人、常规筛查前接受输血的人、透析患者、有多个性伴侣的人、有注射吸毒史的人以及肝功能检查持续异常的人。
事件
关于 乙型肝炎病毒 (HBV) 感染的指导文章强调早期筛查的重要性,以防止无声进展为肝硬化、肝功能衰竭和肝癌。本文作者为孟买富通医院肝病学和肝移植手术高级顾问 Vikram Raut 博士。
规模、影响与风险
In India, approximately 300 million people are estimated to be living with Hepatitis B or C without knowing they are infected. HBV and HCV infections often progress silently for years, causing serious liver damage before symptoms appear. Early symptoms are vague and easily dismissed, leading to delayed diagnosis. HBV can be controlled with antiviral medications, and HCV can often be cured with oral medicines. HBV is preventable through vaccination, while HCV transmission is primarily through infected blood.
应对、状态与不确定性
该文章强调需要提高认识和早期筛查,特别是在高危人群中。它推荐针对 HBV 的 疫苗接种 和减少 HCV 的安全医疗实践 传播。证据基于专家评论和一般医学知识;所提供的证据中没有详细说明具体的 暴发 调查、测试方案或官方公共卫生应对措施。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

How early screening can beat 'silent killers' Hepatitis B and C

News Brief
A leading Indian hepatologist has underscored the critical role of early screening in combating Hepatitis B virus (HBV) infections, which often progress silently for years before causing severe liver damage such as cirrhosis or cancer. Dr. Vikram Raut, a senior consultant at Fortis Hospitals Mumbai, notes that 300 million Indians may unknowingly live with HBV or Hepatitis C, with symptoms like fatigue or nausea frequently misattributed to stress or lifestyle factors. HBV is preventable via vaccination and controllable with antiviral therapy, while Hepatitis C—transmitted mainly through contaminated blood—can often be cured with short-course oral medicines. The article urges high-risk groups, including healthcare workers and dialysis patients, to seek screening, warning that delayed diagnosis dramatically increases the risk of life-threatening complications.
Original source text (English)
How early screening can beat 'silent killers' Hepatitis B and C Symptoms of these critical infections, which can lead to cirrhosis and liver cancer, are vague and easily dismissed Hepatitis B and C often progress silently for years, causing serious liver damage long before any warning signs appear. Dr Vikram Raut, senior consultant-hepatology and liver transplant surgery at Fortis Hospitals Mumbai, explains why early screening is the key to preventing cirrhosis, liver failure and liver cancer. Most people believe that if they have a serious liver disease, they will develop jaundice, severe pain or obvious symptoms. That is not true for Hepatitis B and Hepatitis C. These infections are often called “silent killers” because they can quietly damage the liver for years without causing any noticeable symptoms. This is largely attributed to the huge reserve and regeneration potential of the liver—we just need 30 per cent of the normal liver for body functions. In India, 300 million people are living with Hepatitis B or C without knowing they are infected. Many discover the disease only when they develop liver cirrhosis, liver failure or even liver cancer. One of the biggest reasons these infections are ignored is that the early symptoms are vague and easily dismissed. A person may experience mild tiredness, reduced appetite, occasional nausea, bodyaches or weakness. These symptoms are commonly attributed to stress, lack of sleep, ageing or a busy lifestyle. Since there is usually no pain, fever or jaundice in the early stages, people rarely think about getting their liver checked. Another challenge is the lack of awareness. Many people believe Hepatitis spreads only through alcohol consumption or poor food hygiene. In reality, Hepatitis B can spread through infected blood, unprotected sexual contact or from an infected mother to her baby during childbirth. Hepatitis C is primarily transmitted through infected blood, such as unsafe injections, unsterile medical or dental procedures, tattooing with contaminated equipment or sharing needles. Many infected individuals cannot identify exactly when or how they acquired the infection. The encouraging news is that both infections can be detected with a simple blood test. Hepatitis B can be effectively controlled with modern antiviral medications, helping prevent liver damage. Hepatitis C, in most cases, can now be completely cured with oral medicines taken for a few months. Early diagnosis dramatically reduces the risk of cirrhosis and liver cancer. Certain individuals should be particularly vigilant. These include people with a family history of Hepatitis B, healthcare workers, individuals who received blood transfusions before routine screening became widespread, patients on dialysis, people with multiple sexual partners, those with a history of injecting drug use, and anyone with persistently abnormal liver function tests. Hepatitis B is also preventable through vaccination. Every newborn should receive the Hepatitis B vaccine, and adults who have never been vaccinated should discuss immunisation with their healthcare provider. Although there is currently no vaccine for Hepatitis C, safe medical practices and blood screening have significantly reduced new infections. The message is simple: do not wait for symptoms to appear. If you belong to a high-risk group or have never been screened, ask your doctor about Hepatitis B and C testing. A few minutes spent on a blood test today could prevent life-threatening liver disease tomorrow. When detected early, these silent infections can be controlled and in the case of Hepatitis C often cured, allowing people to live long and healthy lives
View five news elements
Time
Published on 2026-08-06; no specific event date reported in the supplied evidence.
Location and population
India; general population, with emphasis on high-risk groups such as healthcare workers, individuals with a family history of Hepatitis B, those who received blood transfusions before routine screening, dialysis patients, people with multiple sexual partners, individuals with a history of injecting drug use, and anyone with persistently abnormal liver function tests.
Event
Guidance article on Hepatitis B virus (HBV) infection emphasizing the importance of early screening to prevent silent progression to cirrhosis, liver failure, and liver cancer. The article is attributed to Dr. Vikram Raut, senior consultant in hepatology and liver transplant surgery at Fortis Hospitals Mumbai.
Scale, impact and risk
In India, approximately 300 million people are estimated to be living with Hepatitis B or C without knowing they are infected. HBV and HCV infections often progress silently for years, causing serious liver damage before symptoms appear. Early symptoms are vague and easily dismissed, leading to delayed diagnosis. HBV can be controlled with antiviral medications, and HCV can often be cured with oral medicines. HBV is preventable through vaccination, while HCV transmission is primarily through infected blood.
Response, status and uncertainty
The article highlights the need for increased awareness and early screening, particularly among high-risk groups. It recommends vaccination for HBV and safe medical practices to reduce HCV transmission. The evidence is based on expert commentary and general medical knowledge; no specific outbreak investigation, testing protocols, or official public health response is detailed in the supplied evidence.

耻辱感如何助长乙型肝炎流行区 - standardmedia.co.ke

How stigma fuels Hepatitis B in endemic zones - standardmedia.co.ke
新闻简报Mercy Kahenda 于 2026 年 8 月 1 日在肯尼亚 Standard Media 发表的文章“耻辱如何在流行区助长乙型肝炎”探讨了巴林戈县乙型肝炎患者所面临的社会耻辱和歧视。它突显了 16 岁的纳尔逊 (Nelson) 所经历的情感和心理负担,他目睹了自己与家人和朋友的关系发生了巨大变化。这篇文章强调了耻辱如何影响乙型肝炎患者的生活,特别是在该病毒流行的地区。虽然本文没有提供具体病例数或统计数据,但它强调需要提高认识和理解,以应对与该疾病相关的社会挑战。该分析基于个人叙述,不包括详细的公共卫生应对措施或干预措施。

时间
发布于 2026-08-01,事件详细信息参考了发生在 16 岁时的 诊断(大约 2026-05-01)。
地点与对象
肯尼亚巴林戈县,一名家庭成员(尼尔森)及其母亲和哥哥也患有乙型肝炎。
事件
被诊断患有乙型肝炎的人经历了来自社区的社会耻辱和歧视,他们之间互动的变化以及本文重点关注耻辱对流行区乙型肝炎的影响就证明了这一点。
规模、影响与风险
该文章强调了耻辱对乙型肝炎患者的社会和心理影响,但没有报告具体的病例数、死亡人数或暴露人数。
应对、状态与不确定性
文章讨论了耻辱对更广泛的社会和公共卫生的影响,但没有提供有关测试、隔离或接触者追踪等具体应对措施的详细信息。来源是新闻文章,信息基于个人账户。

How stigma fuels Hepatitis B in endemic zones - standardmedia.co.ke

News Brief
The article 'How stigma fuels Hepatitis B in endemic zones' by Mercy Kahenda, published on August 1, 2026, by Standard Media in Kenya, explores the social stigma and discrimination faced by individuals living with Hepatitis B in Baringo County. It highlights the emotional and psychological burden experienced by Nelson, a 16-year-old diagnosed with the virus, who saw his relationships with family and friends change dramatically. The piece emphasizes how stigma affects the lives of those with Hepatitis B, particularly in regions where the virus is endemic. While the article does not provide specific case counts or statistical data, it underscores the need for greater awareness and understanding to combat the social challenges associated with the disease. The analysis is based on a personal narrative and does not include detailed public health responses or interventions.

Kiprono took three months before disclosing her Hepatitis B status to neighbours. [Mercy Kahenda, Standard] Discrimination & Identity Relations In the quiet rhythm of family life in Baringo County, Nelson once moved without fear. He shared meals, laughter, and closeness with those he loved. Then, at sixteen, everything changed. Diagnosed with Hepatitis B—the same virus that had already taken his mother and elder brother—he woke to a new, painful reality. Overnight, plates, spoons, and cups became instruments of separation. ALGS Stock Rallies 16% After-Hours On $25M Cash Boost From China Hepatitis B Drug Deal - Stocktwits

ALGS Stock Rallies 16% After-Hours On $25M Cash Boost From China Hepatitis B Drug Deal - Stocktwits
新闻简报Aligos Therapeutics (ALGS) reported a significant financial boost following a licensing agreement with Xiamen Amoytop Biotech. The company received a $25 million upfront payment, providing non-dilutive funding to advance its pipeline of treatments for liver and viral diseases. Under the terms of the deal signed earlier this year, Xiamen Amoytop Biotech holds exclusive rights to develop and sell the experimental hepatitis B treatment, pevifoscorvir sodium, within Greater China. The agreement allows Aligos to remain the rights holder in the United States, Europe, Japan, South Korea, and other global markets. Financial incentives for Aligos include potential milestone payments totaling up to $420 million and tiered royalties in the high single digits on net sales in the licensed region. The treatment has already achieved Breakthrough Therapy Designation from Chinese regulators, potentially accelerating the approval process. A joint steering committee is currently overseeing the program, and the partners intend to begin clinical testing for another Aligos drug candidate in China during the third quarter of this year.
查看新闻五要素
时间
报道于2026-08-05;周一注意到股市走势;今年早些时候签署了许可协议。
地点与对象
大中华区(独家开发及销售权);Aligos Therapeutics(公司);厦门淘拓生物科技(合伙人)。
事件
Aligos Therapeutics announced receipt of a $25 million upfront payment from its Chinese licensing partner, Xiamen Amoytop Biotech, under a deal granting exclusive rights to develop and sell the experimental hepatitis B treatment, pevifoscorvir sodium, in Greater China.
规模、影响与风险
The $25 million upfront payment provides non-dilutive funding for Aligos's pipeline. The deal includes potential milestone payments of up to $420 million and tiered royalties in the high single digits on net sales in the licensed region.
应对、状态与不确定性
联合指导委员会正在监督该计划;治疗 已获得中国监管机构的突破性疗法认定;合作伙伴计划于今年第三季度在中国开始另一款 Aligos 候选药物的临床测试。

ALGS Stock Rallies 16% After-Hours On $25M Cash Boost From China Hepatitis B Drug Deal - Stocktwits

News Brief
Aligos Therapeutics (ALGS) reported a significant financial boost following a licensing agreement with Xiamen Amoytop Biotech. The company received a $25 million upfront payment, providing non-dilutive funding to advance its pipeline of treatments for liver and viral diseases. Under the terms of the deal signed earlier this year, Xiamen Amoytop Biotech holds exclusive rights to develop and sell the experimental hepatitis B treatment, pevifoscorvir sodium, within Greater China. The agreement allows Aligos to remain the rights holder in the United States, Europe, Japan, South Korea, and other global markets. Financial incentives for Aligos include potential milestone payments totaling up to $420 million and tiered royalties in the high single digits on net sales in the licensed region. The treatment has already achieved Breakthrough Therapy Designation from Chinese regulators, potentially accelerating the approval process. A joint steering committee is currently overseeing the program, and the partners intend to begin clinical testing for another Aligos drug candidate in China during the third quarter of this year.
Original source text (English)
$ALGS Highly undervalued, cash infusion (non-dilutive) $25M vs $35M MC Analyst TG $38.50 High institutional holder 55% marketbeat.com/stocks/NASDA... 2 10 588.3K Advertisement|Remove ads. The payment gives Aligos non-dilutive funding as it advances its pipeline of treatments for liver and viral diseases. Shares of Interactive Strength Inc. rose on Friday (Representative image: Getty Images) - Under the licensing deal signed earlier this year, Aligos granted Xiamen Amoytop Biotech exclusive rights to develop and sell its experimental hepatitis B treatment drug, pevifoscorvir sodium, in Greater China. - Under the deal, Aligos is eligible for up to $420 million in milestone payments tied to clinical, regulatory, and sales achievements. - Aligos Therapeutics had $54.9 million in cash, cash equivalents and short-term investments as of March 31, 2026. Shares of Aligos Therapeutics (ALGS) rose 21% in after-hours trading on Monday after the company announced it had received a $25-million upfront payment from its Chinese licensing partner. The payment gives Aligos non-dilutive funding as it advances its pipeline of treatments for liver and viral diseases. Aligos Therapeutics had only $54.9 million in cash, cash equivalents, and short-term investments as of March 31, 2026. Under the licensing deal signed earlier this year, Aligos granted Xiamen Amoytop Biotech exclusive rights to develop and sell its experimental hepatitis B treatment, pevifoscorvir sodium, in Greater China. Aligos keeps full rights to the drug in the United States, Europe, Japan, South Korea, and all other markets. Under the deal, Aligos is eligible for up to $420 million in milestone payments tied to clinical, regulatory and sales achievements. It will also earn tiered royalties in the high single digits on net sales in the licensed region. The treatment has already received Breakthrough Therapy Designation from Chinese regulators, which can speed up the approval process. A joint steering committee between the two companies is now overseeing the program. As part of the broader collaboration, the partners also plan to start clinical testing in China of another Aligos drug candidate in the third quarter of this year. On Stocktwits, retail sentiment around ALGS stock jumped from ‘neutral’ to ‘bullish’ over the past 24 hours, while message volume stayed at ‘high’ levels. A Stocktwits user opined that the stock is highly undervalued. “Cash-in now, but the real read is whether this deal de-risks the story and adds validation for the asset. Market will watch follow-through on the platform, not just the headline,” another user wrote. ALGS stock has fallen by about 37% year-to-date. (Photo by Smith Collection/Gado/Getty Images) - Negative wagers against Hertz have surged to an all-time high as bearish investors capitalize on the company's severe market downturn. - The massive surge in short interest was triggered after the rental car company's stock plummeted by roughly 60% in less than 14 days. - The steep sell-off follows a disappointing preliminary second-quarter earnings report, a sudden capital-raising push via a new stock issuance, and a concurrent bond offering. Hertz Global Holdings (HTZ) share price dropped 3.3% on Monday to a record low, marking the ninth straight session of declines. The drop has intensified short-selling pressure on the stock, data from S3 Partners showed. The dramatic drop was set off by a multi-pronged financial update that severely disappointed Wall Street. Hertz issued a profit warning and reported preliminary second-quarter adjusted corporate EBITDA that missed consensus analyst expectations. Following the announcements, the stock plunged a record 41% in a single session—marking its steepest one-day decline since its 2021 post-bankruptcy relisting—before continuing its downward trajectory. As the stock rapidly devalued, bearish traders moved in quickly to maximize their positions. As of Thursday's close, more than 56% of Hertz’s float has been borrowed by traders betting on its stock price falling further, S3 Partners data showed as reported by Bloomberg. “Short interest has increased dramatically,” said Ihor Dusaniwsky, managing director at S3 told Bloomberg. Hertz has seen an additional 13.7 million shares shorted — worth $30 million — since June 24, according to Dusaniwsky. That means about 20% of short positions have been opened in less than two weeks. Hertz stock fell to record lows as concerns about the company’s rising vehicle depreciation costs and weakness in the used-car market have triggered a sell-off. The rental car company also slashed its second-quarter earnings forecast to a range of $50 million to $80 million, citing unexpected weakness in the used car market. Last month, the company also completed a $350 million secured exchangeable notes offering, with an option to raise an additional $50 million, which it said would help boost liquidity and financial flexibility as it advances its turnaround efforts. Retail sentiment on Stocktwits was ‘bullish’ with ‘high’ message volumes. Retail chatter on the stock surged over 1,600% from the previous session. HTZ stock has lost 62% year-to-date. A Rivian R2 pauses during the Rivian Electric Joy Ride experience during the SXSW Conference & Festivals on March 18, 2026 in Austin, Texas. Shares of Rivian Automotive Inc. (RIVN) dropped around 9% in after-hours trading on Monday despite the company reporting preliminary second-quarter revenue estimates above Wall Street estimates, as investors focused on the company's intent to sell 75 million shares in a public offering. Rivian said it intends to use the net proceeds from the offering for general corporate purposes, including funding certain equity contribution requirements tied to its loan arrangement with the U.S. Department of Energy. The equity raise comes as Rivian advances its R2 platform launch, expands manufacturing capacity in Georgia, and progresses on autonomy and commercial vehicle initiatives. In a separate filing, the company provided preliminary consolidated revenue estimates of $1.55 billion to $1.65 billion for Q2 2026, up from $1.30 billion in the year-ago quarter. The increase was driven primarily by higher vehicle volumes, partially offset by a higher mix of lower-priced commercial vans. Rivian also estimated cash, cash equivalents, and short-term investments at approximately $5.3 billion as of June 30, 2026, up from $4.8 billion at the end of the first quarter, owing to investments from partners Volkswagen Group and Uber Technologies. Full second-quarter financials are now slated for July 30. Last week, Rivian reported that it delivered 12,194 vehicles in the second quarter of 2026, exceeding its own guidance range of 9,000–11,000 units. The company produced 12,613 vehicles during the period. Strong demand for R1 trucks and SUVs, continued commercial van sales, and initial customer deliveries of the new R2 SUV contributed to the beat. The company subsequently also raised its full-year 2026 delivery guidance to 65,000–70,000 vehicles, sending shares 8% higher on Thursday. On Stocktwits, retail sentiment around RIVN stock stayed within the ‘bullish’ territory over the past 24 hours, while message volume remained at ‘high’ levels. A Stocktwits user expressed optimism for the upbeat revenue guidance, sending the shares rallying on Tuesday. Another user voiced hopes for the company’s strengthened balance sheet. A third user framed the offering as essential to the company’s plans to advance R2 SUV production. Another, however, raised concerns about dilution. RIVN stock has gained 2% year-to-date. Read More: SpaceXAI Drops New Logo — xAI Fully Merges Into SpaceX SpaceX acquired xAI on February 2, in a record-setting all-stock deal that made xAI a wholly owned subsidiary of SpaceX. The SpaceX logo is displayed on a smartphone screen with the xAI logo in the background.(Photo by Samuel Boivin/NurPhoto via Getty Images) - The new logo and the official rechristening were announced in a post on X. - The changed logo has yet to be reflected on the company’s website or in official filings. - In its IPO prospectus, SpaceX laid out what it called the largest actionable total addressable market in history at $28.5 trillion, of which $26.5 trillion is attributed to AI. Elon Musk's artificial intelligence startup xAI rebranded as SpaceXAI and unveiled a new logo to reflect its full integration into SpaceX on Monday. Shares of SPCX closed down 1% on Monday ahead of the company joining the Nasdaq-100 index before market open on July 7. Musk confirmed in May that his artificial intelligence startup xAI will be dissolved as a standalone company and fully integrated into SpaceX, rebranding its AI efforts as SpaceXAI. SpaceX had already absorbed xAI in a formal acquisition earlier this year, but the new “SpaceXAI” framing highlights that all AI development will now operate directly under the SpaceX umbrella. Musk also confirmed that the company will have a new logo as well. SpaceX’s previous logo was designed by a New Jersey-based creative agency called RO Studio. xAI’s previous logo, the simple ‘xAI’ wordmark, meanwhile, was designed in-house by one of xAI’s co-founders called Toby Pohlen. The new logo retains parts of the older logos. The updated logo has yet to be reflected on the company’s website or in official filings, suggesting the name is more of a rebrand than an official renaming. It is worth noting that SpaceXAI — the rebranded xAI — is a subsidiary of SpaceX, not SpaceX itself. X, the social media app, is itself a subsidiary of SpaceXAI. SpaceX acquired xAI on February 2, in a record-setting all-stock deal that made xAI a wholly owned subsidiary of SpaceX. xAI investors received 0.1433 shares of SpaceX for every share of xAI, consolidating Musk’s empire and fueling further speculation that the billionaire’s EV company, Tesla, will eventually merge with SpaceX as well. In June, SpaceX debuted on Nasdaq, surging nearly 20% on its first day after the largest IPO in history at $135 per share. In its IPO prospectus, the company laid out what it called the largest actionable total addressable market in history at $28.5 trillion, of which $26.5 trillion is attributed to AI — dwarfing its traditional space ($370 billion) and connectivity ($1.6 trillion) segments. The bulk of the AI opportunity is projected to come from enterprise applications, alongside AI infrastructure, consumer subscriptions, and advertising. On Stocktwits, retail sentiment around SPCX remained in the ‘bearish’ range over the past 24 hours, while message volume remained at ‘extremely low’ levels. A Stocktwits user said that SPCX will plummet to $120 per share. Another user expressed anticipation for SpaceX’s upcoming earnings report. “Key levels, volume, and follow-through will determine whether bulls can regain momentum or if weakness continue,” they wrote. SPCX closed at $160.42 on Monday, still implying a significant 7% premium to its debut price of $150 per share. Editor's Note: An earlier version of this story incorrectly stated that SpaceX, the rocket company, had rebranded as SpaceXAI. This was inaccurate. SpaceXAI is the rebranded identity of xAI, Elon Musk's AI startup, which is a subsidiary of SpaceX. SpaceX itself has not rebranded. Response, status and uncertainty
A joint steering committee is overseeing the program; the treatment has received Breakthrough Therapy Designation from Chinese regulators; partners plan to start clinical testing in China of another Aligos drug candidate in Q3 of this year.

Hepatitis patients such as Fiona (far left) and Lin (third from left) are sharing their experiences to raise awareness and encourage earlier testing and care. (Photos: Gilead Sciences) New: You can now listen to articles. This audio is generated by an AI tool. Brand Studio Brand Studio 06 Aug 2026 08:44AM (Updated: 06 Aug 2026 10:31AM) Bookmark Bookmark Share WhatsApp Telegram Facebook Twitter Email LinkedIn Set CNA as your preferred source on Google Add CNA as a trusted source to help Google better understand and surface our content in search results. Presented by Read a summary of this article on FAST. Get bite-sized news via a new cards interface. Give it a try. Click here to return to FAST Tap here to return to FAST FAST When 60-year-old Lin was taken to hospital with liver failure, he was shocked to discover he had hepatitis B. For Fiona, 34, the diagnosis came much earlier. She found out she was a hepatitis B carrier at 15. Besides coping with early-stage liver cirrhosis, she struggled with the stigma surrounding the disease. At school, she kept the diagnosis to herself and withdrew socially. While their experiences differ, both reflect a wider challenge across Asia, which carries a disproportionate share of the global hepatitis burden. Hepatitis B remains a major public health concern in Asia Pacific, which is home to more than two-thirds of the world’s 260 million people living with chronic hepatitis B. The region also accounts for nearly half of the global hepatitis C burden. Hepatitis causes inflammation of the liver and, when left untreated, can lead to long-term damage. The World Health Organization’s 2026 Global Hepatitis Report said countries need to scale up prevention, diagnosis and treatment to meet its 2030 elimination targets. WHEN INFECTIONS GO UNDETECTED Babies exposed to hepatitis B at birth face a high risk of lifelong infection, making timely birth-dose vaccination critical to reducing mother-to-child transmission. Hepatitis often remains under-detected because many people with hepatitis B or C have no symptoms for years, said Dr Janus Ong, a gastroenterologist, transplant hepatologist and associate professor at the University of the Philippines Manila. “Even when patients are diagnosed, they may not seek treatment because they do not feel unwell,” he said. “That can be dangerous, because untreated hepatitis can progress silently over time to cirrhosis, liver failure and liver cancer.” Dr Ong explained that the prevalence of hepatitis B in Asia Pacific is partly linked to historically high rates of mother-to-child transmission, before vaccination and prevention systems were fully established in many countries. Gaps in awareness, screening and access to treatment have allowed infections to persist across generations. Gilead’s Behind the Silence campaign, in collaboration with the World Hepatitis Alliance, shares the experiences of people living with hepatitis B, C and D to help reduce stigma. Stigma and misconceptions continue to make it difficult for people to speak openly about hepatitis and seek care. One common myth is that hepatitis only affects people with certain behaviours or lifestyles, said Dr Ong. “Such misunderstandings, combined with stigma, can make people afraid to get tested or disclose their status. This delays diagnosis and treatment, and allows the disease to progress silently,” he added. Public education and community-led initiatives can help make hepatitis easier to understand and talk about. Awareness efforts such as Behind the Silence, supported by Gilead Sciences and developed in collaboration with the World Hepatitis Alliance, encourage more open conversations about the disease – an important step towards getting more people tested and treated. BRINGING CARE CLOSER TO PATIENTS The StITCH project is developing a people-centred care model for hepatitis B and C patients in primary healthcare facilities in Vietnam and the Philippines. Integrating hepatitis screening and care into primary healthcare settings could help more patients be diagnosed earlier, linked to treatment sooner and receive care in a setting that feels less stigmatising, said Dr Ong. “It is also far more scalable and sustainable than relying mainly on tertiary hospitals or specialist centres.” StITCH, or Strengthening the Integrated Treatment and Care for Hepatitis, is being implemented in Vietnam and the Philippines to test whether a decentralised, primary care-led model can make hepatitis services easier to access. The programme brings screening, counselling, treatment initiation and follow-up services closer to communities, especially through primary care and district-level facilities. For patients, this can mean shorter travel times, lower out-of-pocket costs and a less intimidating care journey. Early results have been encouraging, said Dr Ong, who leads the StITCH project in the Philippines, a collaboration between Gilead, Harvard Medical School and the University of the Philippines Manila. In the Philippines, more than 24,000 people were screened for hepatitis B. Most people who tested positive were linked to care, with many starting treatment. In Vietnam, more than 85,000 people were screened for hepatitis B and nearly 69,000 for hepatitis C. CLOSING THE ACCESS GAP Gilead Sciences’ Focus programme helps healthcare partners strengthen routine screening, diagnosis and linkage to care for hepatitis B, hepatitis C and HIV. Reaching more patients earlier also requires changes within health systems so that screening and linkage to care become part of routine practice. Mr Derek Spencer, executive director of the Focus programme at Gilead Sciences, said the biggest barriers to early diagnosis of blood-borne viruses are often “structural rather than scientific”. Launched in 2010, Focus is a public health initiative that partners with healthcare institutions to address disparities in screening and care in communities that face high rates of late diagnosis, illness and death from blood-borne viruses. The programme enables healthcare institutions to improve access by making screening part of routine care, in accordance with public health guidelines. This can include using electronic health records to prompt screening, and integrating testing, diagnosis and linkage to a first medical appointment into everyday clinical workflows, particularly for HIV, hepatitis B and hepatitis C in underserved communities. As of December 2025, Focus partners across the United States, Spain and Portugal had performed more than 24 million blood-borne virus tests in communities with a high prevalence of disease. “Many people have to visit different facilities before they are screened or linked to care. At each stage, some patients fall through the cracks,” said Mr Spencer. “By embedding screening into routine, everyday care settings, healthcare organisations can help make the process seamless.” With 2030 approaching, Dr Ong said the priorities are clear: Diagnose more people sooner and move services closer to communities. “Addressing hepatitis is achievable, but only if we act decisively and at scale,” he said. “The tools already exist, but they need to be made accessible through strong policy, financing and primary care delivery.” Visit Behind the Silence to learn more about the experiences of people living with hepatitis in Asia and efforts to support World Health Organization’s 2030 elimination goal. The individuals featured in this story are identified only as Lin and Fiona, based on information provided in videos supplied to CNA. Their full names were not provided. B routine priorityPublic-health news

AusperBio Announces First Patient Dosed in Clinical Study of Investigational HBV Candidate AHB-171 - PR Newswire

News Brief
On 3 August 2026, AusperBio Therapeutics Inc. and its affiliate Ausper Biopharma Co., Ltd. announced via PR Newswire that the first patient has been dosed in a clinical study evaluating AHB-171, an investigational therapy targeting chronic hepatitis B virus (HBV) infection. The announcement marks the initiation of a human trial for the candidate, which is designed to address a major global public health challenge as chronic hepatitis B remains a leading cause of liver disease worldwide. No further details on trial design, location, participant numbers, or clinical outcomes were disclosed in the release.

Inquiries Editorial Bureaus Partnerships Media Inquiries Worldwide Offices Hamburger menu Send a Release ALL CONTACT INFO Contact Us 888-776-0942 from 8 AM - 10 PM ET AusperBio Announces First Patient Dosed in Clinical Study of Investigational HBV Candidate AHB-171 News provided by AusperBio Therapeutics Inc. Aug 03, 2026, 05:30 ET Share this article Share toX Share this article Share toX SAN FRANCISCO, Aug. 3, 2026 /PRNewswire/ -- AusperBio Therapeutics Inc. and Ausper Biopharma Co., Ltd. (together AusperBio), a near-commercial Biopharmaceutical company focused on developing targeted oligonucleotide therapeutics for the treatment of chronic hepatitis B (CHB) and other diseases, today announced initiation of patient dosing in a clinical study evaluating AHB-171, the Company's investigational hepatocyte-targeted small interfering RNA (siRNA) therapeutic candidate for chronic hepatitis B. The milestone marks the advancement of AusperBio's second clinical-stage therapeutic candidate and the first clinical candidate developed using its proprietary Au-HALOTM liver-targeting delivery technology and siRNA platform. Together with the Company's lead clinical program AHB-137, AHB-171 further strengthens AusperBio's hepatitis B virus (HBV) pipeline and expands its portfolio of next-generation oligonucleotide therapeutics. CHB remains a major global public health challenge. According to the World Health Organization (WHO) Global Hepatitis Report 2024, approximately 254 million people worldwide are living with HBV, with approximately 1.2 million new infections each year. While current antiviral therapies can effectively suppress viral replication, achieving a functional cure remains a significant unmet medical need. Dr. Guofeng Cheng, Co-founder and CEO of AusperBio, commented, "Dosing the first patient with our HBV siRNA molecule AHB-171 is a significant milestone for AusperBio and underscores our strong ability to develop proprietary platform technologies and translate them into clinical-stage assets. AHB-171 expands AusperBio's HBV pipeline beyond the lead ASO AHB-137 program and reinforces our strategy of developing differentiated oligonucleotide therapeutics with complementary mechanisms of action for the next-generation therapeutics. We believe this approach will play an important role in achieving a functional cure for chronic hepatitis B. We are grateful to the patients, investigators, and study teams whose partnership has made this milestone possible." Dr. Chris Yang, Co-founder and CSO of AusperBio, added, "AHB-171 integrates our proprietary siRNA discovery capabilities with the Au-HALOTM liver-targeting delivery technology to create a differentiated therapeutic candidate. It is designed to provide potent and sustained suppression of viral gene expressions, with the potential to support combination strategies toward a functional cure for chronic hepatitis B. This milestone marks the first opportunity to evaluate its clinical profile of AHB-171 in CHB patients also assess the performance of our Au-HALOTM liver-targeting delivery and siRNA technology platform. We look forward to generating clinical data that will guide the continued development of AHB-171, strengthen our HBV pipeline, and further validate the broader potential of our targeted delivery technologies." About AHB-171 AHB-171 is an investigational hepatocyte-targeted siRNA therapeutic candidate being developed for the treatment of chronic hepatitis B. Leveraging AusperBio's proprietary Au-HALOTM liver-targeting delivery platform, AHB-171 is designed to selectively reduce viral gene expression and provide potent and sustained antiviral activity. As the first clinical candidate developed using the Au-HALOTM platform, AHB-171 is expected to provide the clinical validation of the Company's targeted delivery technologies and siRNA platform while expanding its portfolio of differentiated HBV therapeutics. About AusperBio AusperBio is a near-commercial biopharmaceutical company with operations in the USA and China, dedicated to advancing oligonucleotide and targeted delivery technologies for transformative therapies, with an initial focus on achieving functional cure for CHB infection. The Company has established a diversified innovation engine built on its proprietary Med-OligoTM ASO and Au-HALOTM targeted delivery technology and siRNA platforms, enabling the discovery and development of differentiated oligonucleotide therapeutics across multiple modalities. With multiple clinical-stage programs and a growing preclinical pipeline, AusperBio is advancing a backbone-based combination strategy toward a functional cure for HBV while expanding into additional disease areas with significant unmet medical needs.
